# revl 2.0 — implementation roadmap

**Branch:** `v2.0` · **Design:** docs/syntax-2.0.md · **Why:** docs/vision.md ·
**Status:** core complete; hardening & exit-criterion items below

`v2.0` = realms/interception (`ir_version: 2`) **plus** the full-language
proposal in syntax-2.0.md (`ir_version: 3`) — the pure-expression stratum
grown into a self-hosting-capable language without weakening G1–G8.

## IR versioning (the contract)

| ir_version | carries | backends |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | v1 documents | all — frozen, byte-identical |
| 2 | realms/interception (`isolate`/`intercept`) | cordis-py, cordis (TS), cordis-rs, cordis4j, wasm (realm namespaces), cordis-go |
| 3 | full-language (`fn`, `type`, `match`, `extern`, `test`) | cordis-py, cordis (TS), cordis-rs, cordis4j, wasm (typed core tier), cordis-go |

A document's `ir_version` is the *maximum* feature tier it uses. v1 sources
must keep emitting `ir_version: 1` byte-identically (the frozen-reference
invariant); the backend goldens are snapshots, not a freeze — the invariant
there is "never changes unreviewed" (item 73, docs/conformance.md "Golden
policy"), so a golden *may* regenerate with a reviewed diff.

## Done (dependency order as built)

1. ✅ **Lexer** — multi-char operators, 2.0 keywords, template literals.
2. ✅ **Types & data (§2)** — records, generics `[]`, enums, ADTs, built-ins,
   `T?` sugar.
3. ✅ **Functions & expressions (§3.1–§3.5)** — `fn`/`pub fn`, TS-subset
   expression layer, local mutation (`var`/`while`/`for-of`, non-escaping),
   `==`/`===` equivalence, §3.3 exclusion diagnostics.
4. ✅ **`match` (§3.3)** — exhaustiveness-checked ADT eliminator.
5. ✅ **Modules (§1)** — `use`/`pub`, module-private default, import-cycle check.
6. ✅ **Services 2.0 (§5)** — `async fn`, `commutative`.
7. ✅ **Components 2.0 (§4)** — block-effect form, `fail` (L-Raise).
8. ✅ **Host blocks (§6)** — classified `extern … = @ts {} = @py {}`, G8 audit
   surfaces externs/host code transitively.
9. ✅ **`verified` & `test` (§7)** — `revl test` runner, totality tier.
10. ✅ **Migration (§9)** — template literals; legacy `$` forms rejected with a
    `revl fmt --migrate` hint.
11. ✅ **Stdlib surface** — docs/stdlib-2.0.md (`length`/`push`/`slice`/
    `charAt`/`charCodeAt`/`indexOf`/`concat`; persistent semantics; unknown
    method = compile error). Turing-completeness demonstrated by execution.
12. ✅ **Sound typing & null safety** (`542bbad`) — bidirectional checking in
    `src/revl/typecheck.py`: sound where declared, silent where unknown.
    Service/fn call arguments and returns, provide-method signatures from the
    service (A6), `var` type stability, `Bool` conditions, record fields, ADT
    payloads. `null` evicted from expressions — absence is `Opt[T]`
    (`T → Opt[T]` injection allowed; `Opt[T] ↛ T` with an unwrap hint).
    Documented gradual frontier: host-valued objects, single-letter type
    params, the trusted extern boundary (G8).

13. ✅ **ADT construction & tagged Result** (post-review) — `Opt` stays
    host-null/value (`Some(x)`=x, `None`=null; `match` on it is a null-check),
    while `Result` and user variants are **tagged**: construction lowers to an
    `adt` IR node and `match` discriminates by tag. User cases may shadow the
    built-in `Ok`/`Err` (the `type Outcome = Ok(Row) | …` idiom). Coverage:
    cordis-py + cordis (TS) full (executed); cordis-rs full via native
    `enum`/`Option`/`Result`; cordis4j full — user variants (sealed
    interfaces), `Opt` (`Optional`), and built-in `Result` via a generic
    sealed `RevlResult<T,E>` (verified on JDK 21); **wasm** now lowers
    variants/`Opt`/`Result` to a `[i32 tag][i32 payload]` cell in linear
    memory with tag-dispatch `match` (verified on wasmtime). All **five**
    tiers construct and match tagged values, each verified by executing
    emitted code.

14. ✅ **MCP bridge & agent-facing diagnostics** (docs/mcp-bridge.md) —
    `revl mcp serve` (compiler as an MCP server over stdio: `revl_check`,
    `revl_admit`, `revl_audit`, `revl_tools`, `revl_grammar`), `revl mcp
    schema` (services → tools with annotations **derived** from declaration
    *and* implementation, transitively through `fn` calls), `revl mcp import`
    (tools → revl; only an explicit `readOnlyHint: true` avoids `emission`,
    and generated sources compile), plus structured diagnostics
    (`src/revl/diagnostics.py`, `revl compile --json-diagnostics`): stable
    code, guarantee text, expected/actual, hint. 32 tests.

15. ✅ **syntax-2.0 acceptance benchmark** (§10) — two full 30×3 runs with
    DeepSeek V4 Pro, one against the typing-enforced checker and one against
    the pre-typing checker as a control (`bench/results/`, summarized in the
    README). Headline: **sound typing costs models nothing** — typed
    first-pass is equal-or-better within run-to-run variance. The gap the
    runs did expose was one grammar friction (models write the full
    provide-method signature, which the component grammar rejected), and
    that is fixed: optional parameter and return-type annotations are now
    accepted and checked against the service (A6).

16. ✅ **Arithmetic is specified rather than inherited**
    (**docs/arithmetic.md**). Every tier used to compute whatever its host
    computed, and because an IR `bin` node carried no operand type, no backend
    was in a position to do otherwise. Found by *executing* one source on
    every tier rather than checking that each emitter accepts it — the
    portability floor catches a tier that refuses, never one that accepts and
    then means something else.

    - **`/` is true division and yields `Float`**, even on two `Int`s. §0
      decides it: `/` is spelled as TypeScript spells it, and `7 / 2` is 3.5
      there. The checker had typed it `Int` while two tiers produced 3.5 — a
      soundness break, and rust was the tier out of step with the syntax.
    - **`%` is the truncated remainder** and pairs with `div_trunc`; `mod` is
      Euclidean and pairs with `div_euclid`. Both identities
      (`(a div b) * b + (a rem b) == a`) are published as a **law** and
      executed over sixteen sign combinations on every tier. `div_floor`
      deliberately has no remainder partner.
    - **`Int` is 64-bit two's complement and overflow traps.** Never wraps,
      never silently widens; every hosted tier raises `revl: Int overflow`.
      Two tiers needed ports to hold it: wasm moved i32→i64 (values move,
      linear-memory addresses stay i32, slots widened 4→8 bytes) and
      TypeScript moved `number`→`bigint`.
    - **`Float` is IEEE 754 binary64**, with literals (`1.5`, `1e10`,
      `1.5e-3`). Two consequences are recorded rather than glossed: `==` on
      Float is not reflexive (`NaN != NaN`, caveated in syntax-2.0 §3.4), and
      `<` is a partial order.
    - **Division by zero**: a literal zero divisor is a compile error
      (`examples/rejections/arith_zero_divisor.rvl`); a computed one faults
      uniformly. `/` by zero stays IEEE ±infinity, because it is Float.
    - **Structural `==`** was fixed on the way: TypeScript lowered it to JS
      `===` (identity for objects — `{a: 1} == {a: 1}` was *false*), rust
      lacked `PartialEq`, and Go could not compare slices at all.

    `tests/test_cross_tier_execution.py` executes the whole specification on
    every tier each run. What remains is items 11–13 under "Open".

*(nothing at the moment — see Next.)*

## Next

- ✅ **Emission propagation at service boundaries** (surfaced by the MCP
  bridge, shipped): a service declaration is an **upper bound** on its
  providers' effects — a provider may be purer than declared, never less.
  A plain-declared method whose body reaches an emission (through a required
  service, an `emission` extern, or a function that transitively reaches
  one) is rejected with `G4`. This repairs G8 as well: `revl audit`
  enumerates a caller's emissions from the declarations it calls, so an
  under-declared operation made every consumer's audit incomplete.
  Migration (deliberate, IR-breaking): `examples/user_cache.rvl` and the
  demo now declare `emission fn put`, and the reference IR + four backend
  goldens were regenerated. Rejection: `g4_emission_not_declared.rvl`.

- **Typing follow-ups** (frontier shrinkage):
  - ✅ **match-arm checking against the expected type**: arms are now checked
    individually in check-position (`check_ast`), so disagreeing arms (which
    join to an unknown type and previously slipped through) are refused.
  - ✅ **op sweep inside component setup blocks**: the raising type oracle
    (`infer_ir` with a filename) now runs over block-effect setup statements, so
    operator/builtin misuse and non-`Bool` `if`/`assert` conditions are refused
    as in `fn` bodies, and a non-builtin method on a known stdlib value
    (`Str`/`List`/`Bytes`) is rejected. Host-provenance receivers still infer to
    an unknown and stay silent (fenced in docs/contract-errata.md).
  - ✅ **generics instantiation**: a single-uppercase name in a `fn`/`extern`
    signature that is not a declared type is that function's implicit type
    parameter — marked once in `_signature_table`, wildcard only inside its own
    body, and unified against the actual arguments at each call site
    (`collect_tparams`/`unify`/`substitute`). The marker never reaches the IR.
    Unfenced in docs/contract-errata.md. Closing it also closed a *worse*
    unnamed hole: `_is_wildcard` had treated **any** single-uppercase name as a
    parameter, so a declared `type S = A | B` was silently unchecked everywhere
    with no opt-in from the author.
  - ✅ **six accept-but-won't-compile holes closed**, each one a program the
    checker accepted and rust/java refused — missing return, non-exhaustive
    return paths, unchecked call arity, field access *through* an `Opt`
    (`o.n` / `o?.n`, the one that contradicted the README's null-safety
    claim), `Str` indexing, and an unknown `match` case name. Each was
    verified by emitting with the new check disabled and compiling the result
    with `javac` and `cargo`. Three adjacent leaks went with them: the same
    missing-return hole in `provide` bodies, arrow bodies that were never
    type-checked at all, and component-body `fn` calls with no arity check.
  - ✅ **`type X = Y` means what TypeScript means**. It had parsed as a
    one-case variant whose case was named `Y`, so `type Sku = Str` refused a
    `Str` argument while accepting `return Str`. Aliases are now resolved and
    **erased before checking**, exactly where TS also compiles (an existing
    type on the right); where TS errors (an undeclared name) the one-case
    variant reading stands, so `type Status = Pending` is still how an opaque
    nominal is spelled.
  - Also closed this pass: nullary user-ADT constructors now resolve as values
    inside `test` blocks (the block is the same expression scope a `fn` body
    is), and G6 gained its rejection example (`g6_impure_statement.rvl`).
- **Self-hosted front end** — ✅ lexer and expression parser (the 2.0 exit
  criterion, syntax-2.0 §11). `selfhost/lexer.rvl` and `selfhost/parser.rvl`
  compile (`ir_version: 3`) and are cross-checked against the reference
  implementations. The stdlib items the lexer needed (`split`/`join`/`repeat`)
  have graduated to docs/stdlib-2.0.md; only the `Map` *value* type remains
  'Planned'.

  The **point is the differential oracle, not self-hosting** — two independent
  implementations of one grammar forced to agree, where neither is the spec, so
  a disagreement is always a real defect in one of them. A self-hosted
  *compiler* is a deliberate non-goal: the bootstrap cliff would tax the
  iteration speed this project runs on, and the checker is where a `Map` and
  semantic ground truth begin. **Stop after the parser.**

  Coverage, measured rather than asserted:
  - lexer: **345 of 355 `.rvl` files in the repo agree token-for-token, zero
    disagreements**. The 10 skipped are the one remaining gap — `@backend { … }`
    host bodies, which the reference takes as a single brace-balanced token.
  - parser: the pure-expression layer (§3.2) — every expression node kind that
    occurs in real code (9,112 nodes across 286 files). Declarations and
    statements (4,858 nodes, 34 kinds) are deliberately **not** covered; they
    are linear and low-bug-density, so the oracle earns far less there.
  - `tests/test_selfhost_parser.py` compares canonical S-expressions over ~100
    accepted forms, ~27 rejected ones, and a seeded generator over the whole
    grammar (40k expressions during development, 12 × 60 in CI).

  It found three real defects, all in the self-hosted lexer, all invisible to
  the existing corpus test — the lesson being that **a differential oracle only
  covers what its corpus exercises**: `hole` missing from its keyword set;
  `${…}` accepting only a bare identifier where the reference captures raw
  brace-balanced source; and template parts flattened into one `Str` joined by
  `"|"`, so `${a || b}` put the separator into a payload and made the parts
  unrecoverable. The last was found by fuzzing at roughly one in 400
  expressions and by nothing else. Full writeup, including the language and
  tooling frictions the exercise surfaced, in **docs/selfhost-findings.md**.
- ✅ **Merged to `main`** — every commit since lands there directly, and the
  conformance worktrees have been pruned.
- **Backend wave plan** — the per-tier backend roadmap and wave structure (runtime-truth, instance-parametric foundation, new runtimes, emitter cleanups) live in [docs/backends-roadmap.md](backends-roadmap.md).
- **Runtime-truth wave (backends)** — every tier's guarantees are now asserted by *executing* emitted code, not compile-gating or golden text. Landed: the cross-tier realm-conformance gate (`tests/test_realm_conformance.py` — py/ts/rs/wasm pass "equal strings = same realm" at runtime, java `strict xfail` for the cordis4j global-realm divergence pinned in `docs/contract-errata.md`), executed scenario coverage on wasmtime / the real cordis4j jar / cordis, and the single-renderer convergence (ts/rust/java). Executing instead of inspecting caught **two correctness bugs that had shipped**: a wasm nested-allocation silent miscompile (`{inner:{v:11},k:5}`→`1285` not `16`) and a java host-call-in-`fn` non-compile — both now fixed on `main`. Rust reactive isolate-linking (an isolated consumer not activating on its same-realm provider) is now fixed: the emitter applied realm isolation *inside* the plugin closure, after cordis-rs had already evaluated the `Inject` gate on the un-isolated root context. Moving isolation to plug time (`_revl_isolate_ctx`, mirroring the py/ts `plug()` helper) resolves it — a revl emitter fix, not an upstream one: driven directly, the raw cordis-rs/py/ts runtimes all link an isolated same-realm consumer/provider when isolation is placed at the context level, so cordis-rs needed no change.
- **wasm tier** (the performance + enforcement path, DESIGN §8) — realms +
  typed v3 lowering landed (`409e440`): `isolate`/`intercept` → realm-qualified
  import/export namespaces, and `Str`/`Bytes`/`List<T>`/records → a
  canonical-ABI-shaped linear-memory model; variants/`Opt`/`Result` values +
  `match` lower to a `[i32 tag][i32 payload]` cell, `while`/`for` lower to
  native wasm loops, one v3 function can call another (`call $name`),
  `${expr}` templates interpolate Str and Int (`$str_concat` + an `$int_to_str`
  itoa helper), and local arrows (`let f = x => …; f(a)`) are **inlined**
  (beta-reduced with by-value capture snapshots) — arrows can't escape this
  tier, so no funcref/`call_indirect` is needed. The functions tier is now
  Turing-complete on wasm (fib loop-form + Collatz run on wasmtime), and
  essentially every pure `fn` that runs on py/ts/rust also runs on wasm. Still
  rejected with precise `EmitError` (each a deliberate boundary, not a gap):
  `config` blocks (no instantiation-config channel), host builtins
  (`Map`/`Pool`), non-`Int` component service types, interpolating a compound
  value (List/record/variant), *escaping* arrows (a function-typed param isn't
  lowerable), and `let_pattern` in v3 functions.
- **Additional Cordis backends** — the backend contract is small (DESIGN §7:
  install effect with inverse; provide/read keys; reactive refresh), so revl
  can target any Cordis runtime. **cordis-go (sixth tier): DONE** — targets
  third-party `0xdenny218/stc-go` (paper-faithful Go runtime, pinned
  `b3d6788`); emits ir_version 1/2/**3**, executed on real stc-go, and **at 0
  gaps in the conformance matrix** (CI-gated via `go build`). All five
  *language* tiers (py, ts, rust, java, go) are now at **0 matrix gaps**, each
  validated by its real compiler; only wasm has gaps (its i32→i64 port is in
  flight). One cordis-go divergence pinned in `docs/contract-errata.md`
  (teardown ordering vs cordis-rs). **Rust + Java: compile-gated,
  semantics-smoke-tested, and now placement-capable** — as of `e349487` Rust
  has general per-composition proxy/stub codegen (consumes *and* serves across
  a seam) and Java runs on the real reactive cordis4j (provider death →
  withdrawal), both auto-selected by the placement conductor. Details in
  §"Agent, interop & test surface" item 5 and docs/interop-bridge.md:
  - `backends/rust/` → **cordis-rs 0.3.0** (docs.rs/cordis-rs): real `Map`
    host runtime, config `Default` impl, component `await` via `plugin_async`,
    block-effect `setup`/`if`/`fail`, stdlib via `Revl*Ops` helper traits,
    golden + version gate. Gated by `cargo check` (v1, v2 realms, v3,
    method-level compensate, stdlib on Str+List, template braces) and
    `cargo test` executing an emitted stdlib test block.
  - `backends/java/` → **cordis4j** (github.com/1na-ko/cordis4j): real `Map`
    host runtime (`Map.create()` — `new` is reserved), no-arg config-`default`
    ctor, `await` via `AsyncPlugin`, block-effect `setup`/`if`/`fail`
    (`CordisException`), stdlib via typed `revl*` static overloads, golden +
    version gate. Gated by `javac` against the **assumed** cordis4j API in
    `backends/java/stubs/` (see its README), plus a JVM run of an emitted
    test block.
  **Runtime scenarios (the A1/G7 exit criterion):**
  - **Rust — done, against the real cordis-rs runtime**
    (`backends/rust/scenarios/`): G7 LIFO teardown, A8 fail-revert-and-land-
    FAILED, reactive provider/consumer lifecycle (Inject gating + withdrawal
    deactivation, consumer-before-provider teardown), the A1 boundary, and a
    50-iteration concurrent-divert race asserting torn-state freedom. One
    divergence found and pinned: cordis-rs defers a divert during `await`
    until activation completes (see docs/contract-errata.md, "cordis-rs A1
    divergence").
  - **Java — done, against the REAL cordis4j runtime** (cordis4j-core
    compiled from source, `backends/java/scenarios/RunRealScenarios.java`;
    CI clones and compiles it on every push): G7 LIFO teardown, A8
    fail-revert with failure routing to the caller, reactive inject gating
    + withdrawal deactivating the consumer before the provider's effects
    revert (Theorem 63), and the async boundary. Two emitter fixes came out
    of it: composite order (real `composite` disposes in given order) and
    A8 self-revert (`ctx.effect()` scopes are orphans until `apply`
    returns). The stub gate (`RunScenarios.java`) stays as the offline
    fallback.
  ✅ **`Pool` and `Job` are no longer placeholders.** One normative spec
  (`backends/python/runtime.py`, `.. _pool-job-semantics:`) that the TS runtime
  and the rust/java emitters point at, with a drift-guard test asserting all
  four still agree: `Pool` has real bounded capacity (lowest-id acquire,
  release accounting, use-after-close refused, `in_use + available ==
  capacity`), and `Job` is a real cancellable async handle (`TICKS`
  cooperative turns, `cancel()`, and `pending()` — teardown residue made
  countable), all timer-free so CI stays deterministic. Fixing this exposed a
  cross-tier divergence that a test had been *pinning shut*: java's
  `Pool.execute` reported 0 rows affected where the other three reported 1.

  ✅ **java's `await` now actually awaits.** It lowered an A1 boundary to a
  bare `Job.run("x");`, evaluating and discarding the handle, so activation
  returned with the job still in flight — and it could not have been joined
  even in principle, because the emitted runtime typed `Job.run` as `void`
  against a contract that specifies a handle. Both halves moved. Rust never
  had the bug (its `Job::run` is an `async fn`). Proven by running: the
  scenario harnesses assert `Job.pending() == 0` after activation *and* after
  teardown, on the real cordis4j runtime, and reverting the one-line lowering
  makes them fail.

  Remaining minor limits: method-time `await` stays rejected (A1). **Go is
  done** (cordis-go, above); C/C++/Zig await their runtimes existing.
- **Instance-parametric components — phase 1 landed.** Addressing decided
  (supervision-tree). On `main`: a frozen `spawn` IR (instantiation-as-
  acquisition, no new IR step kind), one grammar form (`spawn C with {…}`),
  the G2/G3/G4/G8 rule changes, and a **cordis-py reference that executes**
  (two live instances in distinct local realms, independent LIFO teardown,
  request-scoped early reclamation). See **docs/design-v2-instances.md**
  ("Phase 1 — frozen"). Remaining: the other four tiers' `spawn` lowering,
  and hot-swap with live instances. This subsumes the old "dynamic realms"
  item — the limitation it lifts is stated in
  docs/design-v2-realms.md.
- Minor demo frictions: parameterize runtime import, trace resolved config,
  multi-observer `set_trace`.

## Agent, interop & test surface (ranked)

Five capability questions, answered against the code as it stands. Ordered by
value, not by size. Each names what exists, what is genuinely missing, and the
recommendation.

### 1. ✅ `revl_swap` and in-memory compilation — done

Both gaps closed. `compile_source` now carries the ambient manifest and
`modules` (in-memory sources for `use`), so inline source is compiled without
touching the disk — the temp-file shim is gone, and a virtual source stands in
for the file it names in both root loading and `use` resolution. The server
holds a live composition (`mcp/session.py`, a trace-capturing subclass of
`run._Driver`, so the lifecycle is the same code `revl run` uses and stdout
stays clean for JSON-RPC) and exposes `revl_load`, `revl_call`, `revl_swap`,
`revl_rollback`, `revl_unload`, `revl_state`.

The whole point in one line: **an agent iterates on a component that has no
file, tests it by calling it, and proves it leaves no residue before anything
is written out** — and a rejected candidate cannot deploy, because the compile
runs before the transition, so the running composition keeps serving.

Covered by `tests/test_mcp_session.py` (skips where cordis-py is absent).

### 1b. Self-evolution — done, and what building it exposed

`demo/self_evolve.py` (+ `tests/test_self_evolution.py`) is a composition
that rewrites one of its own components: `Evolver` requires an `Assistant`
(the model, an ordinary coeffect) and a `Runtime` (the compiler, another
ordinary coeffect), asks for a new component, and the runtime admits or
refuses it against what is currently loaded. An admitted candidate becomes
the next generation; a refused one never reaches the queue and the
composition keeps serving. Nothing touches the filesystem.

Two design points that make it safe rather than merely clever: `propose`
**queues** rather than swapping synchronously (a component asking to replace
the composition it belongs to would be asking to be disposed inside its own
method), and the assistant is a **swappable provider** — the stub is
deterministic for CI, and pointing it at a real model is a provider swap,
not a code change.

Building it surfaced three language gaps. Two are fixed:

- ✅ **An emission's return value was unusable.** `emit` was a statement that
  discarded its value — fatal for the canonical emission that *returns* data
  (an LLM completion, an HTTP GET). `emit` is now also an expression prefix,
  so `return emit assistant.complete(p)` and nested forms work while the
  marker stays at the call site (G4's actual point). The G4 upper-bound walk
  was extended to see emissions in value position.
- ✅ **`emit` was rejected on an `emission` extern**, though a service
  emission required it — an asymmetry with no justification. `emit` now
  accepts either.

Both are now fixed too:

- ✅ **A provision key colliding with a host context member** used to compile
  and then die at activation (`provides runtime: …` → `property "runtime" is
  already declared as accessor`). The reserved set is host-specific, so the
  rejection lives in the emitters that know their own surface: the cordis-py
  and cordis (TS) backends reject such a key with the name and the reason.
  The rust/java/wasm tiers do not expose a context object this way.
- ✅ **Provide-method bodies now admit plain bindings.** `let x = <expr>`
  names an intermediate value (`let x = effect … undo …` still means the
  acquisition — the parser branches on the `effect` keyword), `var` plus
  assignment is available where a method needs to accumulate, A3 renaming
  applies, and an activation body still refuses a plain binding with a
  diagnostic pointing at the effect form (G6). Lowered as `let`/`assign`
  steps and implemented across **all five** emitters — python, TypeScript
  and java as native locals, wasm as declared `(local $x i32)` slots, rust as
  `let`/`let mut` in all three of its method paths (its pure path now emits a
  statement sequence instead of requiring a single return).

### 1c. ✅ Cross-tier portability, and the divergences it found

`tests/test_cross_tier.py` runs one source through **all five** emitters and
asserts they agree. It needs no toolchain (every emitter is pure Python), so
the floor is cheap to hold, and a tier that genuinely cannot express
something is listed in `EXPECTED_LIMITS` with its reason — which keeps a
deliberate restriction distinct from an oversight.

Writing it immediately paid for itself. Method bindings had shipped to four
tiers, and closing the rust gap surfaced two more divergences of the same
shape — a construct accepted by some emitters and unknown to others:

- **TypeScript could not render pure 2.0 expressions inside component or
  method bodies.** It carries two expression renderers (one for component
  bodies, one for `fn` bodies) with disjoint kind sets, so `let msg = prefix
  + name` — a pure `bin` beside a `req` call — hit `unknown expression kind
  'bin'`. They now fall back to each other, with the document's v3 context
  published for the duration of `emit`.
- **wasm had the same split**, so i32 arithmetic in a method body was
  refused although it is precisely this tier's native shape. Its component
  renderer now lowers `bin`/`un` to i32 instructions directly.

The lesson worth keeping: every one of these was a *renderer* divergence
rather than a semantic one, invisible to any single-tier test, and each was
found within minutes of running one source through all five.

### 1d. Conformance matrix — the gap list (docs/conformance.md)

`tools/conformance.py` runs 48 constructs through all five emitters and
reports what each tier does. python is the reference: **0 gaps**. The rest,
worst first, deliberate tier limits excluded:

1. ✅ **`??` is unimplemented on rust, java and wasm** — a documented 2.0
   operator that is python-only today (TypeScript has it in `fn` bodies but
   not component bodies). The lowering is well-formed; three operator cases
   are simply missing.
2. ✅ **Calling a pure `fn` from a component body fails on TypeScript and
   wasm** — the `fn` call node is in neither renderer's kind set. This one
   cause is 9 of TypeScript's 12 refusals.
3. ✅ **`match` is not usable in a component or method body** — frontend-level,
   so every tier is equally affected; ADT-consuming components must call out
   to a `fn`.
4. ✅ **A bare `return` does not parse**, though the IR and two emitters already
   model it — the natural body for a void service operation.
5. Tier-local: TypeScript has no `if` step in component bodies (so `fail`
   guards do not lower) and misreads `??` there; java *rejects* a `fn` whose
   name collides with a Java keyword instead of renaming it (A3 already
   solves this for bindings); rust cannot read `config` inside a guard
   expression; wasm's component renderer lacks `if`/`list`/`record`/`fn`.

Every one is a **renderer** divergence — a well-formed node with no case in
some backend — never a semantic one, and all are invisible to single-tier
testing. The structural fix after the immediate gaps: one expression
renderer per backend covering every IR kind, with tier limits as explicit
refusals rather than missing cases. python, the only tier with a single
renderer, is the only tier with no gaps.

### 1e. ✅ Conformance sweep — all real gaps closed

Three agents in parallel (one per backend directory) plus a frontend batch
took the matrix from 12/5/5/32 real gaps to **zero**: python 0, typescript 0,
rust 3, java 3, wasm 19 — and every remaining refusal is a deliberate tier
limit with a one-line justification (docs/conformance.md).

Follow-ups this surfaced, none blocking:

- ✅ **The matrix's blind spot is closed.** It used to check only that an
  emitter did not raise. `tools/validate.py` now *compiles* emitted output per
  tier (`cargo check`, `javac` against the cordis4j stubs, `wasmtime` module
  validation), and a dedicated `conformance` CI job installs node, cargo,
  JDK 21 and a pinned wasmtime so all five tiers are validated on every push —
  previously four of them skipped in CI. `--check-toolchains` fails rather
  than reporting a silently unvalidated tier. The 16 real emitter bugs this
  exposed (3 rust, 13 java, incl. the `requires`-capture bug named here) are
  fixed, not baselined: `KNOWN_FAILURES` is empty. Two of them failed on
  *both* tiers from one upstream cause — host builtins had no declared
  signatures, so each backend carried a private copy and four tiers disagreed
  about a contract nobody enforced; `_HOST_ARG_SIG`/`host_check` now type
  them in the frontend.
- ✅ **The component IR dialect is uniform** (closed by 5d59834's tagged-ADT
  design, re-verified by execution): `Opt` is deliberately host-null/value —
  `Some(x)` lowers to `{kind:"call", callee:{kind:"var", name:"Some"}, args}`
  and bare `Some`/`None` to `{kind:"var"}` in **every** position (fn bodies,
  component setup blocks, provide methods, `test` blocks — asserted by
  compiling one program through all four and diffing the shapes), while
  tagged construction (Result and user variants) always lowers to the `adt`
  node. What each backend still does privately is *emit* that one dialect
  into its host representation (python identity/`None`, go `RevlSome`, …) —
  that is tier emission, not dialect normalization.
- **`call` is ambiguous by kind across dialects** (component `target`/
  `method` vs 2.0 `callee`/`args`), the one node where the two-renderer
  split fails *silently*. A constraint on the single-renderer refactor.
- ✅ **Doc examples are compiled, and the collection floor is guarded.** Every
  fenced ```revl block in README + docs is now checked, under a four-way fence
  convention — `program` (must compile), `fragment` (syntax-checked inside
  scaffolds), `sketch` (must *not* compile, so it fails when the feature lands
  and tells the author to promote it), `reject` (with an optional diagnostic
  code that must match). It was written because all three flagship `UserCache`
  examples had silently stopped compiling when G4 gained its upper-bound
  direction — one of them contradicting a rule stated 70 lines below it. In the
  same pass `tests/test_mcp_session.py` went from collecting **zero** items (a
  module-scope `importorskip` aborted the whole file, taking three runtime-free
  tests with it) to 5 passing, with `tests/test_collection_guards.py` pinning
  that class of bug shut.
- ✅ **The interop seam checks the method, not just the key.** The py and TS
  stubs dispatched any method name off the wire while validating only the
  service key — the allowlist already existed on the proxy side and in the
  placement spec, and simply was not given to the stub. Probes in a placement
  file are now parsed (`ast.parse` + `literal_eval`) instead of `eval`'d with
  full builtins, `--placement` preflights like plain `revl run`, and
  docs/interop-bridge.md states the trust model it had never stated: same-user
  sockets in a 0700 dir, no authN/authZ, values by copy, and what it is *not*
  — not a sandbox, not a multi-tenant boundary.
- **Test-hygiene hazards** (closed, see "Open" item 7): three claims with no
  gate behind them — a cold TypeScript checkout collecting two fewer tests
  than a warm one, the wasm tier's 25 runtime tests skipping whenever
  wasmtime was absent, and the rust cargo gates verifying nothing on a
  machine with no route to crates.io. All three read green while checking
  less, which is the failure mode this section keeps rediscovering: the
  hazard is never a red test, it is a test that was never collected.

### 2. ✅ Lifecycle tests — the paradigm's own guarantee, asserted in-language

**Landed** (syntax-2.0 §7.1, `tests/test_lifecycle*.py`). `lifecycle` is a
**modifier, not a new top-level form** — the precedent is `verified fn`, an
adjective that changes what a body may contain without changing what the
declaration *is*. The grammar delta is one token in one position, which is
what §8's prompt-size requirement demands. A body is a script over a live
composition: `load C with { … }`, `call key.op(…)`, `let x = call …`,
`unload C`, `assert no_residue`.

The assertions bite, which is the whole point: a leaky `undo` is caught via
R1 (`open() with no close()`) and a forgotten `unload` via R4's introspection
delta — both proven by tests that *expect failure*, on the cordis-py
reference tier. The other four tiers refuse a lifecycle test with a named
`EmitError` rather than half-implementing it.

**Was:** `test "name" { assert … }` lowered through the *pure-statement* path
(`_lower_tests` → `_lower_pure_stmt`), so a test could only exercise `fn`s —
teardown correctness, the thing most worth testing in a language about
revertible composition, was expressible only in each backend's own Python/TS
suite. The shipped form:

```revl
lifecycle test "cache reverts cleanly" {
  load UserCache with { }
  call cache.put("k", "v")
  unload UserCache
  assert no_residue          // R4, asserted from inside the language
}
```

Natural companion to `verified`; the backends already implemented every
primitive it needed (R1–R5 suites). Examples: `examples/lifecycle_cache.rvl`,
`examples/lifecycle_leak.rvl`, and seven rejection fixtures under
`examples/rejections/lifecycle_*.rvl`.

### 3. ✅ Cross-tier test execution — `test` is a conformance harness

**Landed:** `revl test <files> [--backend py|ts|rust|java|wasm|all]` reuses
each backend's existing runner — py executes `REVL_TESTS` in-process, ts runs
the emitted vitest blocks, rust runs `cargo test` over a throwaway crate,
java compiles against the cordis4j stubs (or the real classes on
`REVL_CORDIS4J_CLASSES`) and runs `REVL_TESTS` on a JVM, and wasm lowers each
`test` block to an exported zero-arg Bool function (`revl_test_*`, true =
pass, a failed `assert` traps) and invokes every export on the real wasmtime
substrate — host-side, since the substrate has no in-language runner. A tier
whose toolchain is absent is *skipped with a reason* (vitest missing, no
working JDK, crates.io unreachable, no wasmtime binary) — never reported as
passing. `--all`
runs every tier that is present and fails the run if any available tier
fails: every `test` block is now a portability assertion, gated only by the
toolchains in front of you. The cheapest high-value change on this list is
done.

### 4. Import codegen family — wrap what already exists

**Today:** libraries and modules wrap cleanly via `extern` + host blocks, with
multi-backend bodies (`= @ts {} = @py {}`) keeping the component portable.
Binaries wrap by hand in the idiomatic shape — `extern acquire fn spawn(...)
undo kill(...)` plus `emission` for writes — and the subprocess shows up on the
G8 audit. `revl mcp import` is the only *automated* importer.

**Gap:** every other existing interface (OpenAPI service, WIT world, C header)
is hand-transcribed. Note the standing cost, which is by design: an extern is
per-backend and *trusted* — revl types the boundary, never the inside.

**Do:** grow the family — `revl import openapi|wit|mcp`. WIT first: the wasm
tier's types line up one-to-one with WIT worlds (docs/interop-bridge.md), so
it is codegen rather than design.

- ✅ **`revl import wit` landed** (`src/revl/import_wit.py`, docs/import-wit.md,
  39 tests). Hand-written WIT parser, no new dependency. A WIT interface becomes
  a `service` + `extern`s + provider component; records/variants/enums map
  one-to-one, aliases are *inlined* (revl's `type X = Y` declares a variant, not
  an alias), and resources/handles/`stream`/`future`/`flags`/`tuple` are refused
  by name, line and remedy rather than approximated. The emission question is
  settled the way `revl mcp import` settles it: WIT asserts nothing about
  reversibility, so **every operation is `emission` unless a recorded human
  claim says otherwise** (`/// @revl:pure` in the WIT, or `--pure` at import
  time) — and G4 keeps the weakened form honest, since the compiler rejects a
  plain operation whose provider reaches an emission. The types line up as
  predicted; what did *not* is the tier — cordis-wasm is still i32-only, so a
  string/record-carrying world imports to valid revl that only the hosted tiers
  (`--backend py|ts|rust`) can lower today.
- ✅ **`revl import openapi` landed** (`src/revl/import_openapi.py`,
  docs/import-openapi.md, 85 tests) — §4 is now complete. An OpenAPI 3.x document
  becomes a `service` + `extern`s + provider; schemas map to records, enums to
  ADTs, discriminated `oneOf` to a variant, `additionalProperties` to `Map`,
  optional/`nullable` to `Opt`, and recursive `$ref`s need no special case.
  `allOf`/`anyOf`/`not`, untagged `oneOf`, free-form objects, header/cookie
  parameters and non-JSON payloads are refused by name, JSON pointer and remedy.
  This is the first importer where the *document* carries reversibility
  evidence: RFC 9110 §9.2.1 makes `GET`/`HEAD`/`OPTIONS`/`TRACE` safe, so a safe
  verb is the explicit in-band claim that weakens the family's `emission`
  default — while `PUT`/`DELETE` stay `emission` (idempotent is not reversible),
  the generated header records that safe-by-spec is the API author's claim and
  not a checked property, and `--pure`/`--emission`/`x-revl-emission` override in
  both directions with an explicit emission claim always winning. No new
  dependency: stdlib `json`, and YAML delegated to PyYAML if importable rather
  than approximated by a subset parser.

### 5. Service versioning, then (maybe) a component repository

**Today:** `--placement` composes **py ↔ node ↔ rust ↔ java** in one lifecycle,
symmetrically (every tier can both consume and provide across a seam) and
reactively (every tier turns provider death into withdrawal), with all four
verified end to end via `revl run --placement … --once`.
`distributability` is a *checked* verdict — an address-space-bound service is
refused at the seam rather than silently marshalled.

**Symmetry: reached** (`e349487`). Rust both consumes *and* serves across a
seam — `backends/rust/emit.py` generates the proxy/stub/dispatcher per
composition, the placement runner is generic (no composition-specific code),
`placement.py` regenerates `components.rs` from the running IR before
`cargo build`, and the no-serve guard is gone. Java gained a *reactive*
runtime on real cordis4j (JDK 21): a monitor connection turns provider death
into withdrawal, so a consumer unloads reactively (R2/R3) instead of seeing an
exception; `placement.py` auto-selects it when JDK 21 + cordis4j classes are
present and says so in the placement summary, falling back to the stub
otherwise.

Both narrower limits closed in turn (`cab678e`), making **rust a full
reactive participant** alongside py/node/java:

- **Full type marshalling** — the generated proxy/stub marshal any
  non-opaque type through serde (records, ADT enums, `Map`, `Result`, and
  their `List`/`Opt` nestings), not just scalars. Emitted records/enums carry
  serde derives, and the IR `types` dict is threaded through service-trait and
  bridge emission so a record in a signature emits as its concrete struct
  rather than an opaque `Value`. Scalar branches are byte-identical, so the
  golden holds. Fixture: `examples/rec.rvl`.
- **Peer-death monitor** — one monitor thread per proxy holds a connection to
  the provider socket; on EOF it disposes the proxy, so the cordis-rs consumer
  deactivates reactively (Active → Pending, no panic). The hold now
  distinguishes graceful stdin-close from peer death.

**What remains open at the seam:**

- ✅ **ADT/`Result` wire encoding is now cross-language** (`70d0ba6`): the
  tiers agree on one canonical encoding rather than rust's serde-tagged
  form, so an ADT seam is no longer rust↔rust only. This was the "settle it
  once" call flagged here — it was settled.
- **The opaque host `Value` stays unmarshalled**, by design — it is the
  untyped escape hatch, and marshalling it would mean inventing a type for
  something the language deliberately has none for.
- Not every service may cross, by design (async contract + value types) —
  `distributability` refuses the rest at the seam rather than marshalling it
  silently.

**On a repository:** not yet. The admission gate already answers the question
cargo cannot — not "does this version resolve?" but "does this fit *this
running system*?" — and the manifest is already a publishable JSON artifact
carrying load order, coeffects and the G8 boundary. But publishing would force
an unsolved problem prematurely: revl links by **key identity** (`db:
Database`) with no versioning or structural compatibility (paper §6.6). Two
independent authors both providing `db` collide, and a provider that reshapes
`Database` breaks consumers with no semver to catch it.

**Do:** key namespacing + an interface-compatibility check (the admission
gate's drift detection is the seed) **before** any registry. A registry when
there is a second author, not before.

## Open, in rough priority order

Everything above that is not marked ✅, collected — the roadmap is long
enough that the live items were getting lost in the history.

**How this list is maintained** (multiple sessions write this file):
design/product sessions own item text, sequencing, and this preamble;
implementing sessions flip `✅` and append terse `Landed: …` lines with
file/test citations — they do not rewrite entries. On a merge conflict,
the side carrying landed detail wins. New items get the next number;
numbers are never reused.

**The next three waves** (the list below is long; this is its execution
order — each wave's items unblock or feed the next; numbered, not
lettered, because docs/backends-roadmap.md already owns Waves A–D):

- **Wave 1 — evidence & hygiene:** 22 (hygiene), the §4
  attention-readiness bundle, 16 (admission latency), 18 (residue probe),
  25 (placement CI). Cheap, mostly independent, and 18 is the artifact
  the write-up ships on. 24 (threat model) lands here too — before the
  positioning goes public, not after.
- **Wave 2 — the verification spine:** 37 (`prop test`, designed first),
  26 (`verified effect`, its first instance), 30 (fault sweep),
  21 (audit --diff), 33 (boundary policy), then 31 (gauntlet) as their
  umbrella, with 20 (paradigm bench) scoring the result and
  28 (interchange format) carrying the dossier.
- **Wave 3 — the live-system spine:** 23 (live migration, gated on 25),
  27 (runtime why + oracle), 36 (plan/apply, riding 27's oracle),
  15 (persistence), 34 (query time modes), 40 (replay bisect),
  10 phases 2–5. The demo wave: what turns the evidence into the thing

  3: its hint guarantee is Wave 2's story, its live surface is Wave 3's.

Cross-cutting, any wave: 17 (lower.py split, before 10 phase 2),
14 (`import cordis`, when the lighthouse needs its consumer),
35 (formatter, whenever diff noise starts costing review time),
38 (refusal telemetry, whenever a bench run lands new corpus),
50's metric (tokens-to-green joins the bench in Wave 1; its
optimizations ride 31/32/35), 48 (docs-first, an afternoon, any time).
Later than the waves but designed during them: 47 (crash recovery,
extends Wave 3's persistence), 41/42/45 as the ecosystem ring.
**Re-sequenced 2026-08: 49 phases 0/0.5 (structural search, fetch,
reuse-as-a-diagnostic) are unblocked and near-term** — the reuse loop
is the largest single lever on agent token cost (50) and needs only
landed machinery (§5 as predicate, 28's manifests, import/persist);
49's phases 1–2 keep their gates (gauntlet; item 9's namespacing).
Spec-before-code, schedulable any time the relevant surface is next

capabilities) → 56 (network placement — malpractice without 54/55).

**Each wave ends in a tag.** Releases stopped at v2.0.0; tie them to
the waves so every wave closes with something shippable and
announceable: Wave 1 → **v2.1 "evidence"** (probe report published,
CI covers placement, threat model in-tree), Wave 2 → **v2.2 "the
gauntlet"** (a candidate component gets a dossier), Wave 3 → **v3.0
"live systems"** (the cross-tier migration demo runs from a tagged
release). A tag is a claim the wave's exit tests pass. *Status
2026-08-23:* items 18–23, 25, 31, 35, 42, 43 landed within a day of
being written — the waves are being consumed faster than scheduled;
v2.1 is ready to cut as soon as the §4 readiness bundle and item 24
ship, and `revl swap` (23, docs/swap.md) means v3.0's centerpiece
already runs.

**De-risk first — cheap probes before expensive items.** Each probe is
hours-to-a-day and can kill or confirm a much larger investment; run
them early, record the verdict here:

- [ ] **DSH pin check** (gates the lighthouse, 14, 39): does a

- [x] **Inverse-descriptor spike** (gates 47): pick the three effect

  **VERDICT (item 47 built):** a SUBSET — only named boundary ops with captured

  **VERDICT (81c7925):** measured — `"😀".length()` = 1 (py) / 2 (ts, java) /
  4 (wasm) / rejected (go, rust — a UTF-16-surrogate literal-escaping defect;
  their length/slice are already code-point). **DECIDED unit = code points**
  (docs/strings.md); float interpolation = ECMAScript Number::toString. Per-tier
  fix work is enumerated for the string wave (item 51) — cross-cutting IR

  literal-escaper only, ts/java method rewrites, wasm largest (WAT UTF-8 decode).

1. ✅ **Fix what the conformance matrix's second half found.** The blind spot
   itself is closed: `tools/validate.py` compiles or validates every tier's
   emitted output (python `compile()` + scope walk, `tsc`, `cargo check`,
   `javac`, `wasmtime compile`), `tests/test_conformance_validate.py` gates
   it against a shrink-only baseline, and a `conformance` CI job installs
   all five toolchains at once — `tools/conformance.py --check-toolchains`
   fails that job if any tier would silently report `unavailable`, since
   "nothing checked it" must never read as "clean". ✅ **The 16 baselined
   failures are also closed** — the shared `await`/method-time cause was the
   host-builtin contract (host calls untyped in the frontend, so `Job.run(1)`
   passed an `Int` where a name belongs) plus a `T`→`Opt[T]` injection never
   materialized and java generics carrying primitives. `KNOWN_FAILURES` is
   now empty; every tier's emitted code is accepted by its own compiler
   (python 51/51, typescript 51/51, rust 48/48, java 48/48, wasm 32/32).
2. ✅ **One expression renderer per backend.** typescript, rust and java each
   converged their v1/component renderer and their 2.0 `_v3_expr` into one
   function that dispatches the ambiguous `call` kind on *shape*, not kind
   (python and wasm were already single-renderer). Verified behaviour-
   preserving: java and typescript emit byte-identical output across all 51
   conformance constructs; rust changed exactly two (`(Some)(x)`→`Some(x)`
   and a string-literal unification, both improvements that pass `cargo
   check`). The rust refactor also removed a latent field-callee wrong-shape
   read. All five tiers still validate 100% under their real compilers.
3. ✅ **Lifecycle tests** (§2 below) — `lifecycle test` asserts no-residue
   over a *live* composition from inside the language, and `fault test`
   injects a failure and asserts L-Raise/no-residue at a chosen step. Both
   lower and execute on the python tier; the other four refuse loudly.
4. ✅ **Cross-tier `test` execution** (§3) — `revl test --backend …|--all`
   reusing each backend's existing runner; a tier whose toolchain is absent
   skips with a reason, never as passing.
5. ✅ **Uniform component IR dialect** — closed by 5d59834's design and
   re-verified by execution (see the ✅ entry in the conformance-matrix
   section above): `Some(x)`/`None` lower to one shape in every position —
   fn bodies, component setup, provide methods, `test` blocks — and tagged
   construction always lowers to `adt`. Backends emit that one dialect; they
   do not normalize two.
6. ✅ **Explicit generic declaration syntax** — `fn id[T](x: T) -> T` (and
   `pub fn`, `extern`) now parse a `[T, U]` type-parameter list and feed it
   into the same `collect_tparams`/`unify` machinery the implicit form uses:
   an explicit name becomes a type parameter even when the single-uppercase
   heuristic would miss it, unifies at each call site, and its marker never
   reaches the IR (implicit and explicit emit byte-identical IR, pinned by a
   test). Shadowing a declared type (`type S = A | B` then `[S]`) is rejected
   rather than silently reopening the wildcard hole. Provide-methods stay
   annotation-free (separate check path). Docs: docs/generics.md.
7. ✅ **Test-hygiene hazards** — closed, and each one now has a gate rather
   than a convention.
   - *TypeScript*: the emitted `test` blocks are checked in, so a cold
     checkout collects what a warm one does (41 = 41). `tests/
     generated_coverage.test.ts` pins it: the generated module must be
     git-tracked, and it must contain one `it(` per `test` block the fixture
     declares, by name. Checking a file in is a convention; this fails if
     the convention lapses or the emitter quietly stops lowering tests.
   - *wasm*: CI installs a pinned wasmtime, asserts the binary runs, and
     sets `REVL_REQUIRE_WASMTIME=1` — with the flag set, a missing runtime
     is a failure instead of a skip. A junit check then fails the job if
     *any* wasm test skipped, which covers `tests/test_wasm_backend.py` too.
     Local dev without the flag still skips cleanly.
   - *rust*: the cargo gates resolve `--offline` first and fall back to the
     networked resolve only when the offline attempt failed for a
     *resolution* reason. CI (empty registry) fetches as before; an offline
     machine with a warm `~/.cargo` runs all ten gates for real — 26 passed
     in ~43s where the previous skip-if-unreachable rule ran none of them.
     `test_offline_fallback_never_launders_a_real_failure` pins the safety
     property: a compile error, a failing `#[test]` or a panic is never
     reclassified as "retry with network".
   - *(found on the way)* the TypeScript suite's nested `vitest run` sat
     inside vitest's 5s default timeout while spawning node plus six
     `emit.py` processes, so it failed as "Test timed out" under load —
     roughly one run in three, and worse with every file added. The clock is
     now 60s; the assertion is unchanged.
8. ✅ **Import codegen family** (§4) — complete: `revl mcp import`,
   `revl import wit` (39 tests) and `revl import openapi` (85 tests). All
   three default an imported operation to `emission` and weaken it only on
   explicit evidence, because a service declaration is an upper bound on its
   providers (G4) and an under-declared operation breaks every consumer's G8
   audit. OpenAPI is the first member whose document carries real evidence —
   RFC 9110 makes GET/HEAD/OPTIONS/TRACE safe — and the generated header says
   plainly that safe-by-spec is the author's claim, not a proof, and that
   idempotent (PUT/DELETE) is not reversible.
9. ✅ **Service versioning** (§5) before any component repository. **Half
   done:** the admission gate now checks *structural interface
   compatibility* (paper §6.6, docs/service-compat.md) instead of exact
   match — a hot-swap is admitted iff every running consumer's call site
   stays valid (a retained provider pins the interface via A6; a replaced
   one may add methods, widen params, narrow returns, drop an emission,
   but never make one *appear* — that would silently cross the boundary
   for an unmarked consumer, G4/G8). **Deferred:** key namespacing (needs
   surface syntax that collides with active parser work), and
   `plan._interface_drift` still previews drift with a plain `!=`.
10. **Instance-parametric components** — phase 1 landed, and ✅ **PHASE 2 DONE
    ON ALL SIX TIERS:** `spawn` lowering executes on cordis-py (reference), ts,
    rust, go, java, **and wasm** — each proven on its real runtime (the 4 spawn
    properties: distinct local realms, independent LIFO teardown, request-scoped
    anti-leak, supervision-tree addressing). wasm was the hard one — it needed
    **B3** (per-instance local/identity realms), a contained change in the
    first-party `cordis-wasm` runtime (a realm prefix on the flat provider
    table), *not* a module-per-instance; the revl-side emitter is on `main`,
    and the cordis-wasm B3 commit is pushed to the cordis-wasm repo (`f5c0562`).
    ✅ **Instance accessor** — reading a provision back through a handle
    (`s.<key>` field access, no new grammar; frozen `instance-get` IR node in
    docs/design-v2-instances.md) — is landed on **all six tiers** (the fan-out
    included the async-activation fix `0c09da7`: spawn waits for the child to
    activate so the read resolves through the live context). ✅ **Hot-swap**
    with live instances (migrate-state) landed — structural migration is the
    canonical substrate (item 53), the same-typed-reorder hazard named + pinned.
    Subsumes the old "dynamic realms" item.
    **Named horizon — placement-aware spawn:** today `spawn` always lands
    in the spawning process, and placement assigns *components*, not
    instances. Their convergence ("spawn C for tenant X *in process P*",
    and with item 23, migrating a hot tenant's instance to another tier)
    is verified rebalancing — elastic sharding with checked isolation.
    Not scheduled; recorded so phases 2–5 leave room for it: the spawn
    lowering must not bake "same process" into any tier's shape.

11. ✅ **`Int` widens into a `Float` position, and the IR now says where.** The
    last open item in arithmetic — a *wrong answer* on TypeScript, not a
    refusal — is closed. The frontend now marks each coercion site on the
    node (`"widen": "Float"`, additive like `operands`, no `ir_version` bump:
    a coercion site needs a declared `Float` position, which only full v3
    sources can express, so the v1/v2/v3 reference documents stay
    byte-identical), and every backend emits the conversion outright
    (`float(3)`, `Number(3n)`, `(3i64 as f64)`, `float64(3)`,
    `((double) (3L))`). rust no longer refuses with E0308 and TypeScript no
    longer computes the wrong answer; wasm still refuses `Float` as before.

    `compatible("Float", "Int")` is true, so `1.5 + 2` and `ident(3)` (for
    `fn ident(x: Float) -> Float`) both type-check. The `bin` node carries
    `operands`, so the **arithmetic** case was always specifiable; a `call`
    argument, a `let` value and a `return` expression reached a backend as a
    bare `lit`/`var` with no declared type, and the tiers that keep `Int` and
    `Float` apart split:

    | tier | before | now |
    |---|---|---|
    | python, go, java | a host rule absorbed it | explicit conversion emitted |
    | rust | **E0308** — a loud compile error | `(3i64 as f64)` |
    | typescript | **wrong answer** — `3n === 3` is false | `Number(3n)` |

    Pinned positively in `tests/test_cross_tier_execution.py` (the marker is
    asserted present in the IR, each tier's conversion text is pinned, and
    the old `DIVERGENCES` entry is closed with the probe now *passing* on
    every tier that can run it). Written up in docs/arithmetic.md.

    Found by the TypeScript BigInt port, which is the pattern worth noting:
    it was invisible while `Int` and `Float` were both JS `number`, and three
    tiers hid it behind a host rule.

12. ✅ **Sized and unbounded integers** — `Int` is now 64-bit two's complement
    with trapping overflow on all six tiers (docs/arithmetic.md), which
    leaves two named types specified-but-unbuilt, both additive:

    - `Int32`, for density. Measured, not assumed: i32 and i64 scalar
      arithmetic are identical (0.99 vs 0.98 ns/iter here), but a
      *vectorisable* loop is ~34% faster in i32 because SIMD gets twice the
      lanes. That is the case a static `Int32` serves — and the case a
      dynamically-promoting integer could never serve, since a promotable
      array cannot be stored flat.
    - `Integer`, arbitrary precision. Viable everywhere via a tagged
      small-value representation (rust `ibig`/`malachite` store small values
      inline; wasm can tag a pointer and keep the bignum *inside* linear
      memory, so confinement holds). Cost is concentrated in wasm, which
      would need a bignum in WAT.

    Trapping costs ~9% on scalar arithmetic and much more on vectorisable
    loops, where the check defeats auto-vectorisation — which is the argument
    for `Int32` rather than for making the default unsafe.

    Landed: `Int32` is complete on all six tiers (32-bit trapping, coercions
    pinned — implicit widen to `Int`, explicit checked narrow — with 3 rejection
    fixtures). `Integer` is **fenced, not built**: documented in
    docs/contract-errata.md + docs/integer-proposal.md rather than half-shipped
    (the wasm bignum-in-WAT is the concentrated cost). That second half remains.

13. ✅ **A total division form** — `checked_div_trunc`/`checked_div_floor`/
    `checked_div_euclid` and `checked_mod` return `Result[Int, Str]` on all
    six tiers (`Ok` on success, `Err` on a zero divisor or the `Int.MIN / -1`
    overflow), so a program can handle a zero divisor without faulting and
    without extending `fail` into the pure stratum — totality, the property
    `verified` rests on, is preserved. Documented in docs/arithmetic.md
    (total forms table); a literal zero divisor on the partial forms remains
    a compile error and a computed one still faults uniformly.

14. ✅ **`revl import cordis` — a fourth import-family member.** Landed: `revl import cordis` (src/revl/import_cordis.py) scans a Cordis (TS) plugin's `inject`/`provide` surface into revl `extern service` decls, defaults ops to `emission` and weakens only on explicit `@revl:pure`/`--pure` evidence, and refuses unrecoverable signatures loudly (or `// UNRECOVERED` under `--mark-unrecovered`). docs/import-cordis.md, 23 tests. The import
    family (§4: MCP, WIT, OpenAPI) wraps *specified* interfaces. The runtime
    family revl actually compiles for now has a live plugin ecosystem worth
    wrapping: Cordis (TS) plugins — Koishi's marketplace, and since 2026-08
    DeepSeek Harness, whose plugin kernel *is* Cordis. An importer that reads
    a Cordis plugin's `inject`/`provide` surface and generates revl
    `extern service` declarations would let a revl composition consume real
    Cordis/DSH plugins as coeffects. The established rules generalize: an
    imported operation defaults to `emission` and is weakened only on
    explicit evidence (G4 upper bound, same reasoning §4 records for
    WIT/OpenAPI), and the generated header states what is claimed vs.
    proven. The honest hard part is that Cordis plugins are untyped TS —
    recovering signatures and undo semantics is the work, and where a
    signature cannot be recovered the import must say so loudly rather than
    guess (the "nothing checked it must never read as clean" rule applies to
    interfaces too).

15. ✅ **Composition persistence.** Landed: `revl_snapshot`/`revl_restore` (src/revl/mcp/persist.py) serialize the admitted sources + manifest to JSON; restore recompiles every source through `compile_source` and the gate (never rehydrates live objects), raising `RestoreError` on any component a newer checker now rejects. CLI `revl mcp serve --restore SNAPSHOT.json`; docs/persistence.md, 12 tests. The MCP session's evolved composition
    (`mcp/session.py`) is in-memory: an admitted generation does not survive
    a restart, so self-evolution (§1b) is durable only for the life of the
    process. A snapshot/restore of the admitted state — the sources of the
    currently-admitted components plus the manifest, enough to *re-admit*
    them through the same gate on boot, not a pickle of live objects — makes
    an evolved composition a persistent artifact. Restore must replay
    admission (compile + gate), never bypass it: a snapshot taken under an
    older checker must not smuggle a now-rejected component past a newer
    one. The replay accumulator (backends/python/replay.py) already records
    the effect history and is most of the machinery for the state half.

16. ✅ **Admission latency as a measured number.** Landed: `bench/admission_latency.py` measures the in-memory `compile_source` → admission-gate round-trip — median 0.165 ms (gate ~0.017 ms) on a representative component, recorded in bench/results/admission-latency.md with a guard test (correctness + 20 ms regression ceiling). If the gate runs inside an
    agent loop (per-candidate, per-generation — the §1b/lighthouse shape),
    compile-plus-admit time per component is a product property, not an
    implementation detail. `bench/` exists; add a benched figure for the
    in-memory `compile_source` → admission-gate round-trip on a
    representative component, tracked so a regression is visible. One
    number, honestly measured, is also the pitch: "admission costs X ms."

17. ✅ **Split `src/revl/lower.py`.** 192 KB and growing — lowering, the
    admission gate, service compatibility, emission analysis and
    spawn/instance support in one file, ~29% of the frontend. A mechanical
    split (`lower/` package, or `admission.py` / `service_compat.py` /
    `emission_analysis.py` peeled off) before instance-parametric phase 2
    (item 10) lands more weight on it. Behaviour-preserving by construction:
    the full frontend suite and the byte-identical v1 goldens are the gate.

18. ✅ **Residue probe for foreign Cordis plugins — the adoption wedge.**
    `revl run` already *proves* no-residue on exit for revl-authored
    compositions (registry, provisions, disposables, listeners back to
    baseline). Point the same contract at plugins revl did not author: a
    standalone harness that mounts/unmounts **any existing Cordis (TS)
    plugin** N cycles and reports what leaked. The py driver's baseline
    proof is the model, not the code — the probe is new TS-side work
    implementing the same before/after contract against the runtime DSH
    and Koishi plugins actually run on. Why it leads this batch: it
    delivers value with zero language adoption (a lifecycle linter for the
    ecosystem revl compiles for), and it *generates the evidence* items
    19–20 and the eventual paper need — "N plugins × M cycles, X% leak
    listeners" is the evaluation section, the write-up, and the demand
    creation ("the same plugin as a revl component makes this leak a
    compile error") in one artifact. Timing is part of the case: DSH's
    developer preview shipped 2026-08 and the attention window will not
    stay open.

19. ✅ **Fix the cordis-py A8 async-body gap upstream.** The largest open
    errata entry on the flagship tier: an async effect-setup failure runs
    its inverses LIFO with no residue (containment holds) but the fiber
    lands `ACTIVE` instead of `FAILED` — A8's "lands FAILED with the error
    recorded" is dropped for async bodies (docs/contract-errata.md;
    reproduced with hand-built IR, confirmed tier-side, sync bodies
    unaffected). The groundwork is done and the fix belongs in the runtime,
    not the emitter: PR against geohotstan/cordis-py (an upstream follow-up
    path is already noted in the errata), routing an async setup failure to
    the fiber's error slot instead of the auto-dispose guard. The replay
    test already asserts *neutrality* rather than pinning the bug, so it
    flips green when the runtime is fixed. Exit: the errata entry closes
    and the fault-test harness stops reporting the wrong state.

20. ✅ **Bench the paradigm, not just the syntax.** `bench/` measures which
    *revl syntax* models write best (30 specs × {v1, v2, v2host},
    compile-rate and iterations-to-green). Add the variant that measures
    whether *revl itself* earns its keep: the same 30 specs authored as raw
    Cordis TS plugins, scored on **lifecycle correctness** — with the item
    18 residue probe as the oracle — not on compile rate alone (raw TS
    always "compiles"; the question is what it leaks). The deliverable is
    the number the whole pitch rests on: agents converge in K iterations
    under guarantee-naming diagnostics, and Z% of their raw-TS attempts
    carry residue revl would have refused at compile time. Infra exists
    (run.py, the retry-on-diagnostic loop, committed corpora, free
    rescoring); the new work is the TS prompt/scoring path and the probe
    dependency.

21. ✅ **`revl audit --diff`: the authority-drift gate.** `audit --json`
    exists; add a mode that takes a previous audit and **fails when a new
    generation adds boundary crossings** (new emissions, new externs)
    without an explicit ack. This is the second axis of the agent-gate
    story and it is deliberately not admission's job: admission (§5)
    checks *correctness* — every running consumer stays valid — while
    audit-diff checks *authority* — a regenerated component cannot quietly
    widen what it reaches outside the system between generations. Small
    surface (a diff over the G8 boundary table, exit code + named
    additions), sized in days, and it completes what the lighthouse entry
    (Toward early production §3) calls the review surface.

22. ✅ **Hygiene sweep.** Small, all real: the README badge still says
    `runtimes-5` (and the repo description "five verified backends") with
    six backends in-tree; a stray untracked `.html` sits at the repo root;
    `backends/typescript/tests/generated/conformance.ts` has an
    uncommitted working-tree drift that needs a decision (regenerate or
    commit); and ~40 local branches from merged waves want pruning. None
    of it blocks anything — batched here so it stops being invisible.

23. ✅ **Verified live migration across tiers — `revl swap <component>
    --to <backend>` on a running placement.** Placement got the tiers into
    one lifecycle (§5 above: py ↔ node ↔ rust ↔ java ↔ go, symmetric,
    reactive); this makes membership *changeable* while it runs. The
    operation: boot the candidate provider in its own process on the
    target tier, run it through the admission gate against the running
    manifest (§5 structural compatibility — every consumer's call site
    stays valid), re-point the consumers' proxies from the old socket to
    the new one, then tear the old provider down LIFO and prove no
    residue. Every ingredient exists and none are wired to each other:
    hot-swap is py-tier-only (`--watch`, MCP `revl_swap`), placement is a
    static assignment at boot, and the seam's canonical wire encoding
    (`70d0ba6`) means a candidate on another tier already speaks the
    protocol. The genuinely new engineering is the **handover**: today the
    per-proxy monitor thread knows only how to *dispose* on peer death —
    teach it to reconnect to a successor instead, so cutover is a
    re-point, not a withdrawal blip. In-flight calls at cutover: drain the
    old provider, honestly, as v1 — mid-stream handoff is a later
    refinement, not a blocker.

    A second verification axis comes almost free once item 18/replay
    mature: record a timeline against the old provider, re-drive the
    recorded calls against the candidate, and compare canonically-encoded
    results *before* cutover — interface-level safety from admission,
    behavior-level safety from replay, so "the shadow agrees" is a checked
    verdict rather than a dashboard. Separable; ship the swap first.

    Why this outranks its cost: it converts the six-backend investment
    from conformance discipline into the product — the backends stop
    being six ways to deploy revl and become six tiers one live system
    can move between. No other system has the category (Erlang hot
    upgrade is single-language and unverified relative to consumers;
    rolling deploys swap processes with no interface check and no residue
    proof), and it is the strangler-fig migration — the one moment an
    organization willingly adopts new glue — as a verified, *reversible*
    operation. Demo/exit test: a composition serving a live REPL on py;
    `revl swap UserCache --to rust`; the REPL answers across the cutover;
    the old provider unwinds with the no-residue proof printed; then swap
    it back.
 

    **+ upgrade simulation against history (external proposal #12, folded here).** `revl simulate-swap old.rvl new.rvl --history run.jsonl` replays recorded interactions against BOTH versions and compares returned values, capability crossings, lifecycle transitions, teardown, resource usage, error classes. Structural compat says 'it can connect'; simulation asks 'does it behave acceptably on real historical workloads'.

24. ✅ **Threat-model the gate itself; harden it against hostile input.** The
    positioning items 18–21 and the lighthouse entry build on — revl as
    the admission gate for agent-generated components — means the gate
    runs on adversarial input *by definition*. The mindset already exists
    in one place (`tests/test_mcp_hint_adversarial.py` attacks the G4
    read-only-hint proof with emissions hidden behind first-class function
    values); generalize it into a program:

    - **`docs/threat-model.md`** — what the gate defends against, and the
      explicit non-goals stated before someone "discovers" them: `extern`
      host blocks are arbitrary code by design (the gate *surfaces* them
      on the G8 audit surface; it does not sandbox them — the deployer
      reviews that boundary), and admission promises consumer validity and
      lifecycle guarantees, not that a well-typed component is benign.
    - **Fuzz the frontend.** `compile_source` runs inside the harness
      process via the MCP server, so a pathological source (deep nesting,
      adversarial unicode, gigantic literals, degenerate generics) that
      hangs or crashes the compiler is a DoS on the very system the gate
      protects. The bar, property-tested: reject or error cleanly —
      never hang, never crash.
    - **Resource bounds on admission** — source-size caps and compile
      timeouts, so an agent regeneration loop cannot starve the gate.
      Pairs with item 16: the same latency measurement supplies the
      budget.

    Exit: the threat-model doc exists and is linked from the MCP/agent
    docs, and the fuzz suite runs in CI with the same "a skip is loud"
    discipline as the rest.

25. ✅ **Placement in CI — prerequisite for item 23.** `ci.yml` runs no
    placement job: the cross-tier seams (py ↔ node ↔ rust ↔ java ↔ go)
    are exercised only where a local machine happens to have the
    toolchains, so "verified end to end" is not yet "verified on every
    commit". The conformance job already installs all five toolchains;
    add a `revl run --placement … --once` smoke across the seams to the
    same job. Sequenced **before** work on item 23 starts — otherwise
    every handover bug will be indistinguishable from a pre-existing seam
    regression nothing was guarding.

26. ✅ **`verified effect` — pay the spec's own debt.** The language's biggest
    honest concession (docs/vision.md; docs/replay.md §4.1): the checker
    proves an inverse is *present and well-shaped*, never that it is
    correct — "an `undo` that is wrong, partial, or a no-op" is explicitly
    not caught. syntax-2.0 §7 already *specifies* the answer (`verified
    effect` — the checker must find or generate the evidence that the undo
    undoes); the §7 ✅ above covers only the totality tier for pure `fn`,
    so this is specified-but-unbuilt with no item tracking it. Until now.

    The buildable middle between "undecidable in general" and "nothing":
    **inverse round-trip testing**. For a `verified effect`, the test
    runner auto-generates the property check — snapshot observable state,
    run the effect, run the inverse, assert the state fingerprint matches
    — N randomized rounds, on the real runtime. Not a proof, and the
    report must say so (the same honesty rule as OpenAPI's "safe-by-spec
    is the author's claim"): a test the author did not write, upgrading
    "trust the author's undo" to "this undo survived N round-trips".
    Scope honestly per replay §4.1: in-process observable state only;
    aliased references, external effects and clock-derived values stay
    out of reach and the generated test's header names them. Why it
    outranks everything nearby: it converts the paradigm's weakest link
    into a checked tier, it is what makes replay's step-back trustworthy
    rather than merely mechanical, it feeds item 18 (a foreign plugin's
    disposal can be round-tripped the same way) — and it is the feature
    Cordis-the-library structurally cannot have, because only a compiler
    that knows which expression is the effect and which is the inverse
    can generate the test.

27. ✅ **Runtime "why" — causal lifecycle traces, and prediction-vs-actuality
    as a new oracle.** The `withdraw` query is an *exact prediction* (G2
    uniqueness + G3 acyclicity: no "maybe it resolves elsewhere"), and
    why-traces explain compile-time rejections — but the runtime trace
    records events with no causes: when a cascade actually fires, nothing
    in the `revl run` stream says *UserCache unloaded because Database
    withdrew because the provider process died*. Two halves:

    - **Causal traces**: every lifecycle transition in the run/trace
      stream carries its cause chain, queryable post-hoc
      (`revl why <component> --trace run.jsonl`). The system explains
      itself — possible here and nowhere else because the dependency
      graph is a checked artifact, not an APM's guess.
    - **The oracle**: the static query predicts the exact cascade set and
      order; the causal trace records the actual one; diff them. Every
      real withdrawal becomes a free conformance check that the runtime
      does what the compiler computed — the differential-parser-oracle
      move (selfhost-findings.md), applied to the runtime. A disagreement
      is always a real defect in one of them.

28. ✅ **Publish the manifest + audit JSON as a versioned interchange
    format.** DESIGN §10's own open question, unanswered since it was
    written: "How much of the linker's manifest should be a stable,
    documented format? (It is the natural interchange point with agent
    harnesses that want to admit components at runtime.)" The lighthouse
    positioning makes the answer urgent: with a versioned schema and a
    documented compatibility promise, **other tools consume the gate's
    verdicts without running revl** — a harness reads what a component
    provides/requires/reaches as a standard artifact. The work is small
    (the JSON exists; schema + versioning promise + docs) and it
    compounds items 14, 18 and 23. The strategic point, stated plainly:
    whoever publishes the format for "verified component" defines the
    category.

29. ✅ **Realm erasure report — compliance as a compiler artifact.** Every
    ingredient exists separately: realms isolate tenants
    (examples/tenants.rvl, correct on every tier including the cordis4j
    fix), `withdraw`'s `survivors` set answers "did this touch the other
    tenant" exactly, the lifecycle machinery proves no-residue on unload,
    and `audit` enumerates boundary crossings with compensation status.
    Compose them into one command — `revl erase-report --realm <r>` — and
    the output is an auditor-facing artifact: in-process state provably
    gone (the no-residue proof), every boundary crossing the realm's
    components made listed as compensated vs. bare, other realms provably
    untouched (`survivors`). The report states its own honest scope in
    its header (compensation is not inversion, paper §6.1 — external
    observers already saw the data; the report *enumerates* that
    exposure, it does not undo it). Nobody generates right-to-erasure
    evidence from a type system today; this is a report generator over
    machinery that already works.

30. ✅ **Fault sweep — from "fail at a chosen step" to "fail at every
    step".** A `fault test` today injects failure at one author-chosen
    point (`fail at step 3` / `fail at effect db`) and asserts
    L-Raise/no-residue/LIFO/siblings there. The compiler already knows
    the complete step list from the IR, so the enumeration is mechanical:
    auto-generate the injection at **every step and every effect**, run
    the full assertion set at each, on every tier that can execute. A
    component with N steps gets N fault tests the author never wrote,
    and the claim upgrades from "A8 holds at the point I thought to
    check" to "**no mid-life failure point exists where this component
    leaves residue**" — an exhaustive verdict, which is what the paper's
    evaluation and the lighthouse pitch both want ("we don't sample
    failure points; we sweep them"). Nearly free: the fault-test
    machinery does the hard work; the sweep is a generator loop plus a
    report that names any step it could not reach. Horizon, recorded not
    promised: seeded clock/random coeffects and async-interleaving
    exploration are the full deterministic-simulation version of this —
    worth building only once async component bodies are common; the
    sequential sweep captures most of the value now.

31. ✅ **`revl_gauntlet` — the proving ground as one MCP verb.** Today the
    agent loop is binary: `revl_swap` admits or refuses. The gauntlet
    makes it graded: **candidate in → dossier out**. One verb that takes
    a candidate component and, in an isolated scratch session the live
    composition never sees, runs the full battery — the admission gate
    (§5), a lifecycle test (no-residue over a real boot/unload), the
    item 30 fault sweep at every step, and item 26's inverse round-trips
    — then returns a structured verdict dossier separating what was
    *proved* (admission, derived teardown), what was *tested* (sweep,
    round-trips, with counts), and what remains *claimed* (the G8 extern
    boundary, enumerated). The sentence that is the feature: admission
    proves a candidate *may* run; the gauntlet proves it *does* run
    correctly — before it touches anything. Sequencing is honest: this
